Group Actions from Algebraic Flops
Caroline Namanya

Abstract
. This paper constructs derived autoequivalences associated to an algebraic flopping contraction X → X con , X\to X_{\operatorname{\mathrm{con}}}, where X X is quasi-projective with only mild singularities. These functors are constructed naturally using bimodule cones, and we prove these cones are locally two-sided tilting complexes by using the local-global properties and a key commutative diagram. The main result is that these autoequivalences combine to give an action of the fundamental group of an associated infinite hyperplane arrangement on the derived category of X . X. This generalises and simplifies [ DW3 ] , by finally removing reliance on subgroups, and it also lifts many other results from the complete local setting.



1.
Introduction

Over the years, studying autoequivalence of derived categories of coherent sheaves has been of increasing interest in algebraic geometry. In
[
DW1
]
using a new invariant
the noncommutative deformation algebra
called the contraction algebra associated to any contractible rational curve in any 3-fold, a particular derived autoequivalence associated to a general flopping curve was described.

More generally, consider a global flopping contraction
X\to X_{\operatorname{\mathrm{con}}}
as in Setup
, where
X
has only mild singularities. After passing to the complete local setting by taking the formal fibre (see
\lx@sectionsign\ref{specific hyperplanes}
), by
[
DW3
]
we can associate two combinatorial objects: a finite hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{H}
and an infinite hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{aff}}.
The question is whether these
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{aff}},
which are local objects, still control the derived autoequivalences of the global
X.
The intuition, and main result of
[
DW3
]
, is that there is a subgroup
K
of the fundamental group of the complexified complement
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{C}^{n}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})
which acts on the derived category
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{coh}X).
This is problematic since
[
N
]
proves that
K\neq\pi_{1}
in general.

The main point of this paper is that the subgroup
K
is not important, and indeed we prove that it is possible to construct a full action of
\pi_{1}
directly. A hint of how to do this appears in
[
A
]
, where bimodule cone autoequivalences are constructed using contraction algebra technology. It turns out that this vastly generalises.

1.1.
Main results

Before stating our main results, all the hard work in this paper goes into establishing the Zariski local situation. As such, consider first an algebraic flopping contraction
U\to\Spec R,
where
U
has only mild singularities (see Setup
2.2
). It is well known, even in this algebraic setting by
[
VdB
]
, that
U
is derived equivalent to some
R
-algebra
\Lambda.
As explained below, we will use bimodule cones on
\Lambda
to construct two-sided tilting complexes. This will then induce derived autoequivalences of
U,
as follows.

Theorem 1.1

(=
6.13
,
)
.

Consider an algebraic flopping contraction
U\to\Spec R
as in Setup
2.2
, with associated finite hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{H}
and infinite hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{aff}}.

(1)

For every wall crossing in
\mathcal{H}
or
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{aff}},
there exists an autoequivalence of
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{coh}U).

(2)

There exists group homomorphisms

In addition to the subgroup
K
defined in
[
DW3
]
being problematic, the generalisation to the algebraic setting comes with two costs, namely there is lack of Krull-Schimdt (since
R
is not complete local), and there are no obvious algebraic objects which correspond to the chambers of
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{aff}},
and so no obvious way of producing autoequivalences via wall crossing.

With Theorem
1.1
in hand, all results globalise. Now, let
h\colon X\to X_{\operatorname{\mathrm{con}}}
be a global 3-fold flopping contraction as in Setup
. As explained in
\lx@sectionsign
, in this global setting there is a product of finite arrangements
\mathfrak{H}
and a product of infinite arrangements
\mathfrak{H}^{\mathsf{aff}}.
We remark that the following generalises
[
DW3
, Theorem 1.2]
in two ways: in the finite case
\mathfrak{H}
it removes the assumption that the curves are individually floppable, and the infinite case
\mathfrak{H}^{\mathsf{aff}}
is new. The following is our main result.

.

1.2.
Bimodule Constructions

Here we briefly explain the construction of the functors in the image of
m
and
m^{\mathsf{aff}}
in Theorem
1.2
. After reverting to the formal fibre in the sense of
\lx@sectionsign
2.3
, there is an associated hyperplane arrangement which depends on Dynkin data (see
\lx@sectionsign
2.3
and
[
IW7
]
). Given a wall
i
in some chamber
D
we can find an atom
\upalpha
from a fixed chamber
C_{+}
to
D
followed by monodromy around wall
i.
One example is illustrated in the following diagram

C_{+}
D
\upalpha

We then associate an endomorphism algebra
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}
to the chamber
D.
This algebra inherits
n+1
primitive idempotents
e_{i},
(for more details see Setup
3.1
), and there is a ring homomorphism
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}\to\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i},
where
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}=\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}/(1-e_{i}).

For
f\colon U\to\Spec R
an algebraic flopping contraction of Setup
2.2
, to construct an autoequivalence on
U
associated to the above monodromy, we consider an endomorphism algebra
\Lambda
that is derived equivalent to
U,
as in §
2.1
. For any choice of
(\upalpha,i)
as above (see also Setup
3.1
), we first define a functor diagram

and then construct a natural transformation
\Uppsi_{\upalpha,i}\circ\Uppsi_{\upalpha,i}^{\operatorname{RA}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}_{\Lambda}
by exhibiting a certain bimodule map

\Lambda\to{}_{\Lambda}\hat{\Lambda}\otimes\left(\mathbb{M}\otimes\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes Z_{\upalpha,i}\right)\otimes\hat{\Lambda}_{\Lambda},

in the derived category of
\Lambda
-
\Lambda
bimodules. Thus taking the cone in the derived category of
\Lambda
-
\Lambda
bimodules gives a triangle

C_{\upalpha,i}\to{}_{\Lambda}\Lambda_{\Lambda}\to{}_{\Lambda}\hat{\Lambda}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\left(\mathbb{M}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}Z_{\upalpha,i}\right)\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}_{\Lambda}\to C_{\upalpha,i}[1].

Define
\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}_{\upalpha,i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}({}_{\Lambda}C_{\upalpha,i_{\Lambda}},-)
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}^{*}_{\upalpha,i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}-\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\Lambda}C_{\upalpha,i_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}}.
The following is the main technical result.

Theorem 1.3

(=
5.3
)
.

{}_{\Lambda}C_{\Lambda}={}_{\Lambda}C_{\upalpha,i_{\Lambda}}
is a two-sided tilting complex, giving rise to the autoequivalence
\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}_{\upalpha,i}
of
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda).

where the top functor is a composition of mutation and Morita equivalences.

The commutative diagram above intertwines the complete local monodromy (in the top functor) with the Zariski local autoequivalence
\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}_{\upalpha,i}.
In case of the finite arrangement
\mathcal{H},
these twist functors also behave well with respect to the contraction algebra equivalences of August
[
A
]
. As notation, let
\Lambda_{\operatorname{\mathrm{con}}}
be the contraction algebra of
[
DW1
]
.

Corollary 1.4

(=
5.5
)
.

For any choice of
(\upalpha,i)
in the finite arrangement
\mathcal{H}
the following diagram commutes

\operatorname{D}(\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{con}})
\operatorname{D}(\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{con}})
\operatorname{D}(\Lambda)
\operatorname{D}(\Lambda)
\scriptstyle\mathrm{J}_{\upalpha,i}
\scriptstyle\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}_{\upalpha,i}

where
\mathrm{J}_{\upalpha,i}
are the compositions of the standard equivalences of
[
A
]
, recalled in
\lx@sectionsign\textnormal{\ref{section key commutative diagram}}.

The key point of Theorem
1.3
is that it is much more general. Contraction algebras only exist in the finite
\mathcal{H},
whereas the autoequivalences in the infinite
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{aff}}
in Theorem
1.3
are much more general.

Conventions

R
is a normal isolated cDV singularity. We will drop both super and subscripts from the tensors as much as possible, whilst still maintaining their natural meanings and contexts in the background. When tensoring by bimodules, we will suppress the obvious module structure, so for a bimodule
{}_{\Lambda}X_{\Gamma},
we write
\otimes_{\Lambda}\Lambda_{\Gamma}.

2.
Preliminaries

The following section gives definitions, terminologies and notation that will be used.

2.1.
Geometric setup

Recall that a projective birational morphism
f\colon X\to Y
is small if the exceptional locus is has a codimension at least two. When the dimension is three, this translates into
f
does not contract a divisor.

Definition 2.1
.

A flop is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{+}}

where
f^{\pm}
are small projective birational morphisms, and the canonical bundles
\omega_{X^{\pm}}
are trivial over
Y
.

We will refer to
f^{-}
and
f^{+}
as flopping contractions. For threefolds, a flop is a process of cutting out rational curves
C_{i}
and replacing them with a union of other rational curves,
without contracting any divisors.

Setup 2.2
.

[
DW3
, Setup 2.3]

Suppose that
f\colon U\to\Spec R
is a flopping contraction which is an isomorphism away from
precisely one point

\mathfrak{m}\in\operatorname{Max}R
. We assume that
U
has only Gorenstein terminal singularities. As notation, above
\mathfrak{m}
is a connected chain
C
of
n
curves with reduced scheme structure
C^{\operatorname{red}}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}C_{j}
such that each
C_{j}=\mathbb{P}^{1}

Under Setup
2.2
, by
[
VdB
, Theorem A]
there is a tilting bundle
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{O}_{U}\oplus\mathcal{N}
on
U
inducing a derived equivalence

\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{coh}U)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}(\mathcal{V},-)}\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda),

where
\Lambda\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{End}_{U}(\mathcal{V})\cong\operatorname{End}_{R}(f_{*}\mathcal{V})=\operatorname{End}_{R}(R\oplus f_{*}\mathcal{N}),
by
[
VdB
, 4.2.1]
.

2.2.
General hyperplane arrangements

A real hyperplane arrangement, written
\mathcal{H},
is a finite set of hyperplanes in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Such an arrangement is called Coxeter if it arises as the set of reflection hyperplanes of a finite real reflection group.
\mathcal{H}
is simplicial if
\cap_{H\in\mathcal{H}}H={0}
and all chambers in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\mathcal{H}
are open simplicial cones. All Coxeter arrangements are simplicial, but the converse is false.

Definition 2.3
.

[
AW
, Definition 2.6]

Let
\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}}
be the oriented graph associated to the hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{H}
defined as follows. The vertices of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}}
are the chambers of
\mathcal{H},
i.e. the connected components of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathcal{H}
. There is a unique arrow
a\colon v_{1}\rightarrow v_{2}
from chamber
v_{1}
to chamber
v_{2}
if the chambers
are adjacent, otherwise there is no arrow.

By definition, if there is an arrow
a\colon v_{1}\rightarrow v_{2}
, then there is a unique arrow
b\colon v_{2}\rightarrow v_{1}
with the opposite direction of
a
. For an arrow
a\colon v_{1}\rightarrow v_{2}
, call
s(a)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}v_{1}
the source of
a,
and
t(a)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}v_{2}
the target of
a
.
A positive path of length
n
in
\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}}
is a formal symbol

p=a_{n}\circ\ldots\circ a_{2}\circ a_{1}

whenever there exists a sequence of vertices
v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}
of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}}
and arrows
a_{i}\colon v_{i-1}\rightarrow v_{i}
in

\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}}
. Set
s(p)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}v_{0},t(p)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}v_{n}
, and call
l(p)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}n
the length of
p.
If
q=b_{m}\circ\ldots\circ b_{2}\circ b_{1}

is another positive path with
t(p)=s(q)
, we consider the formal symbol

q\circ p\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}b_{m}\circ\ldots\circ b_{2}\circ b_{1}\circ a_{n}\circ\ldots\circ a_{2}\circ a_{1}

and call it the composition of
p
and
q
.

Definition 2.4
.

[
HW
, Definition 2.6]
A positive path is called minimal if there is no positive path in
\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}}
of smaller length, and with the same endpoints. The positive minimal paths are called atoms. A positive path is called reduced if it does not cross any hyperplane twice.

2.3.
Specific hyperplane arrangements

Returning to the setting of
2.2
, set
\mathcal{R}=\hat{R}
to be the completion of
R
at the unique singular point
\mathfrak{m}.
The natural morphism
R\to\hat{R}=\mathcal{R},
induces the following diagram

\mathcal{U}
U
\Spec\mathcal{R}
\Spec R
\scriptstyle\upvarphi
2.2
\scriptstyle f

(2.A)

where
\upvarphi
is called the formal fibre. The morphism
\mathcal{U}\to\Spec\mathcal{R}
is a formal flopping contraction, and for a generic element
g\in\mathcal{R},
slicing induces the following diagram

\mathcal{X}
\mathcal{Y}
\mathcal{U}
\Spec(\mathcal{R}/g)
\Spec\mathcal{R}
\scriptstyle\uppsi
\scriptstyle\upvarphi

By Reid’s general elephant
[
R1
]
,
\mathcal{R}/g
is an
\mathrm{ADE}
Kleinian singularity,
\uppsi
is a partial resolution morphism, and
\mathcal{X}
is the minimal resolution. By the McKay correspondence, the exceptional curves
C_{i}
of
\mathcal{X}
are indexed by vertices
i
in a Dynkin diagram
\Updelta.
We will write
\mathcal{J}\in\Updelta
for the curves that are contracted to
\mathcal{Y},
and so
\mathcal{J}^{c}=\Updelta\setminus\mathcal{J}
are those that survive.

The Dynkin data
(\Updelta,\mathcal{J})
gives rise to two hyperplane arrangements. One
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}}
is finite and the other
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathsf{aff}}
is infinite. Both live inside
\mathbb{R}^{|\Updelta|-|\mathcal{J}|}.
The hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}}
is calculated by restricting all the positive roots of
\Updelta
to the subset
\mathcal{J}^{c}.
To calculate
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathsf{aff}},
given a restricted root
a=(a_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{J}^{c}},
the hyperplane
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{J}^{c}}a_{i}x_{i}=0
appearing in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}}
gets translated over the set of integers to give an infinite family
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{J}^{c}}a_{i}x_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}.
This is repeated on every restricted root to give
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathsf{aff}},
for full details see e.g
[
NW
,
IW7
]
. Note that when
\mathcal{J}=\emptyset,
that is
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y},
then
\mathcal{H}
is the finite
\mathrm{ADE}
root system and
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{aff}}
is the extended affine root system.

Example 2.5
.

(\Updelta,\mathcal{J})
with

where by convention
\mathcal{J}
are the unshaded nodes, in the diagram below the full figure is the affine hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{aff}}_{\mathcal{J}}.
Zooming in to only the pink lines is the finite hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{J}}.

When
\mathcal{H}
is simplicial, we write

\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigcup\limits_{H\in\mathcal{H}}H_{\mathbb{C}},

where
H_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complexification of a hyperplane
H.
We then denote the fundamental group of the complexified complement by
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{C}^{n}\backslash\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})
or
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{C}^{n}\backslash\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{aff}}_{\mathbb{C}}),
for
\mathcal{H}
or
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{aff}}
respectively.

2.4.
Mutation

As motivation for finding derived equivalences of a ring, the idea of mutation is to find new tilting modules from old ones, by removing one indecomposable summand of a module and replacing it with another.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a complete local cDV singularity. A module
N\in\operatorname{mod}\mathcal{R}
is said to be maximal Cohen–Macaulay(
=\mathrm{CM}
) if

\mathrm{depth}_{\mathcal{R}}N\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathrm{inf}\{i\geq 0\mid\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{R}/\mathfrak{m},N)\neq 0\}=\mathrm{dim}\mathcal{R},

and we write
\mathrm{CM}\mathcal{R}
for the category of
\mathrm{CM}\mathcal{R}
-modules. Further,
N\in\operatorname{mod}\mathcal{R}
is said to be reflexive if the natural morphism
N\to N^{**}
is an isomorphism, where
(-)^{*}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(-,\mathcal{R}),
and we write
\operatorname{ref}\mathcal{R}
for the category of reflexive
\mathcal{R}
-modules.

Definition 2.6
.

A module
N\in\operatorname{ref}\mathcal{R}
is called a modifying module if
\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{R}}(N)\in\mathrm{CM}\mathcal{R},
and
N\in\operatorname{ref}\mathcal{R}
is called a maximal modifying module(
=\mathrm{MM}
) if it is modifying and

\mathrm{add}N=\{A\in\operatorname{ref}\mathcal{R}\mid\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{R}}(N\oplus A)\in\mathrm{CM}\mathcal{R}\}.

If
N
is
\mathrm{MM},
then
\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{R}}(N)
is called a maximal modification algebra(
=\mathrm{MMA}
).

We next summarize mutation, following
[
IW2
]
. For
C,D\in\operatorname{mod}\mathcal{R},
a morphism
g\colon D_{0}\to C
is called a right (
\mathrm{add}D
)-approximation if
D_{0}\in\mathrm{add}D
and

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(D,D_{0})\xrightarrow{.g}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(D,C)

is surjective. The left (
\mathrm{add}D
)-approximation can be defined dually. Given a modifying
\mathcal{R}
-module
N,
with an indecomposable summand
N_{i},
consider

(1)

a right
\left(\mathrm{add}\tfrac{N}{N_{i}}\right)
-approximation of
N_{i},
namely
V_{i}\xrightarrow{a_{i}}N_{i}

(2)

a right
\left(\mathrm{add}\tfrac{N}{N_{i}}\right)^{*}
-approximation of
N^{*}_{i},
namely
U^{*}_{i}\xrightarrow{b_{i}}N^{*}_{i}

which give exchange sequences

0\to\ker a_{i}\to V_{i}\xrightarrow{a_{i}}N_{i}\quad\mbox{and}\quad 0\to\ker b_{i}\to U^{*}_{i}\xrightarrow{b_{i}}N^{*}_{i}.

(2.B)

From this

(1)

the right mutation of
N
at
N_{i}
is defined by

\upnu_{i}(N)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{N}{N_{i}}\oplus\ker a_{i},

i.e remove the indecomposable summand
N_{i}
and replace it with
\ker a_{i}.

(2)

The left mutation of
N
at
N_{i}
is defined by

\upmu_{i}(N)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{N}{N_{i}}\oplus(\ker b_{i})^{*}.

As in
[
W1
, Appendix A]
, write

\upphi_{i}\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{R}}(N))\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{R}}(\upnu_{i}N))

for the associated derived equivalence
\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{R}}(N)}(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(N,\upnu_{i}N),-).

2.5.
Affine Auslander-McKay bijection

We will consider the general situation of Setup
2.2
where we input a flopping contraction
f\colon U\to\Spec R
with terminal Gorestein singularities, with formal fibre
\mathcal{U}\to\Spec\mathcal{R}.
In this case set
N\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f_{*}\left(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{U}}\oplus\mathcal{N}\right),
where
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{U}}\oplus\mathcal{N}
is the
[
VdB
]
tilting bundle on
\mathcal{U}.
Complete locally we have the following diagram,

\mathcal{U}
\mathrm{(n~curves)}
\Spec\mathcal{R}

Using same notation as in
[
IW7
]
, we will write
\mathrm{MM}^{N}\mathcal{R}
for those modifying reflexive
\mathcal{R}
-modules that have a two-term approximation by
\mathrm{add}N
and have the same number of indecomposable summands as
N
. Further, write
\mathrm{MMG}^{N}\mathcal{R}
for those in
\mathrm{MM}^{N}\mathcal{R}
which have
\mathcal{R}
as a summand. By the general version of affine Auslander-McKay correspondence see e.g
[
IW7
, 0.18]
,
\mathrm{MM}^{N}\mathcal{R}
coincides with the mutation classes of
N
and
\mathrm{MMG}^{N}\mathcal{R}
coincides with the Cohen-Macaulay mutation classes of
N,

and there are the following bijections

\{\mbox{MM}^{N}\mathcal{R}\}
\{\mbox{Chambers of $\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{aff}}$}\}
\{\mbox{MMG}^{N}\mathcal{R}\}
\{\mbox{Chambers of $\mathcal{H}$}\}
\scriptstyle 1\colon 1
\scriptstyle 1\colon 1
\subseteq
\subseteq

under which wall crossing corresponds to mutation. As notation, write
N_{D}
for the element in
\mathrm{MM}^{N}\mathcal{R}
corresponding to chamber
D.

.

2.5
, the following green chamber (or alcove) corresponds to
N
and has 3 summands (corresponding to the three walls). We can mutate to obtain all elements of
\mathrm{MM}^{N}\mathcal{R}
by beginning in the green chamber.

Bounding the above figure to following the pink box illustrates the chambers in the finite arrangement
\mathcal{H}.
We can obtain all elements of
\mathrm{MMG}^{N}\mathcal{R}
(which correspond to the chambers within the pink box) by repeatedly mutating all summands except
\mathcal{R}.

3.
Preliminary Lemmas

Returning to Setup
2.2
, consider the flopping contraction
U\to\Spec R
with formal fibre
\mathcal{U}\to\Spec\mathcal{R}.
As explained in §
2.1
,
\Lambda=\operatorname{End}_{R}(R\oplus f_{*}\mathcal{N}),
thus after completion

\mathcal{R}\oplus\widehat{f_{*}\mathcal{N}}=\mathcal{R}^{\oplus a_{0}}\oplus N_{1}^{\oplus a_{1}}\oplus\ldots\oplus N_{n}^{\oplus a_{n}},

for some
a_{i}\in\mathbb{N},
and so

\hat{\Lambda}=\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{R}^{\oplus a_{0}}\oplus N^{\oplus a_{1}}\oplus\ldots\oplus N^{\oplus a_{n}}).

Set
\operatorname{\mathrm{A}}=\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{R}}(N)
with
N=\mathcal{R}\oplus N_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus N_{n},
and write that
\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}R\otimes_{R_{\mathfrak{m}}}R_{\mathfrak{m}}\otimes\hat{R}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Setup 3.1
.

Given a choice of an atom
\upalpha\colon C_{+}\to D
in
\mathcal{H}
or
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{aff}}
and any wall
i
of
D
we next associate a functor to
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\Lambda).

Note that
\operatorname{\mathrm{A}}
is the basic algebra Morita equivalent to
\hat{\Lambda}
, by e.g.
[
DW3
, 2.6]
. Consider
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{R}}(N_{D}),
where
N_{D}
is described in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{affine auslander }.
Now,
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}
inherits
n+1
primitive idempotents
e_{i}
corresponding to the walls of
D.
For purposes of notation, write
\Upphi_{\upalpha}
for composition of mutation functors corresponding to the path
\upalpha,
and set
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}/({1-e_{i}}).
Consider the composition

\uppsi_{\upalpha,i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i})\xrightarrow{}\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}})\xrightarrow{\Upphi_{\upalpha}}\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{\mathrm{A}})\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{D}(\hat{\Lambda})\xrightarrow{}\operatorname{D}(\Lambda)

(3.A)

where the first is induced by the ring homomorphism
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}\to\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}
, the second is the composition mutations, the third is the Morita equivalence as in
[
DW1
, 5.4]
and above, and the fourth is induced by the ring homomorphism
\Lambda\to\hat{\Lambda}
.

Thus, given information of
(\upalpha,i),
which is complete local since
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{aff}}
are constructed complete locally, we have obtained a functor
\uppsi_{\upalpha,i}
between the derived categories of algebraic objects.

Given that
\upphi_{\upalpha}
is the composition of standard equivalences, we may write
\upphi_{\upalpha}\cong\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}({}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{A}}}\uptau_{\upalpha},-)
for some
\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}
-
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}
bimodule
\uptau_{\upalpha}.
Similarly, write
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}({}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\mathbb{M},-)
for the Morita equivalence between
\operatorname{\mathrm{A}}
and
\hat{\Lambda}.
We have the following result.

Lemma 3.2
.

There is a functorial isomorphism

\uppsi_{\upalpha,i}\cong\begin{cases}\!\begin{aligned} &\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\Lambda}\left(\hat{\Lambda}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\mathbb{M}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}^{\bf{L}}\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i},-\right)\\
&-\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}}\left(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}\otimes_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}^{\bf{L}}\,\uptau^{*}_{\upalpha}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\mathbb{M}^{*}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}\right)\end{aligned}\par\end{cases}

(3.B)

Proof.

Consider the functor diagram

\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i})\xrightarrow[-\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}_{i}}{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}]{\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}}({}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}\!\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i},-)}\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}})\xrightarrow[-\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}{\,\uptau^{*}_{\upalpha}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}]{\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}({}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{A}}}\uptau_{\upalpha},-)}\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{\mathrm{A}})\xrightarrow[-\otimes^{\phantom{L}}_{\!\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}{\mathbb{M}_{\hat{\Lambda}}^{*}}]{\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}({}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\mathbb{M},-)}\operatorname{D}(\hat{\Lambda})\xrightarrow[-\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\,\hat{\Lambda}^{\phantom{L}}_{\Lambda}]{\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\hat{\Lambda}}({}^{\phantom{L}}_{\Lambda}\!\hat{\Lambda},-)}\operatorname{D}(\Lambda).

The left most and right most functors are restriction of scalars, and so in both cases the top functor equals the bottom one. The middle functors are equivalences, so again in each case, the top functor is isomorphic to the bottom functor, now by e.g
[
R3
, p.44]
. The statement follows since (
3.B
) is the composition. ∎

Lemma 3.3
.

\uprho\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}\otimes_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}^{\bf{L}}\,\uptau^{*}_{\upalpha}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\mathbb{M}^{*}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}\right)\cong\mathbb{F}\Upphi_{\upalpha}\left(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}\right)
as right
\Lambda
-modules.

Proof.

Observe that
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}\mapsto\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}\mapsto\Upphi_{\upalpha}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i})\mapsto\mathbb{F}\Upphi_{\upalpha}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i})\mapsto\mathbb{F}\Upphi_{\upalpha}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i})
under the composition defining
\Uppsi_{\upalpha,i}
. Thus

\mathbb{F}\Upphi_{\upalpha}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i})\cong\Uppsi_{\upalpha,i}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{01 of 4th november 2022}}}{{\cong}}\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}_{i}}\uprho\cong\uprho

as right
\Lambda
-modules.
∎

Claim 3.4
.

\mathbb{F}\Upphi_{\upalpha}(S_{i})
is a finite length module supported only at the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
.

Proof.

Since
\upalpha
is an atom, by torsion pairs (see e.g
[
HW
,
\lx@sectionsign 5
]
),
\Upphi_{\upalpha}(S_{i})
is a module or a shift of a module, in particular
\Upphi_{\upalpha}(S_{i})
is in a single homological degree. Thus, since
\mathbb{F}
is a Morita equivalence,
\mathbb{F}\Upphi_{\upalpha}(S_{i})
is also in a single homological degree. Since
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}
is a finite dimensional algebra filtered by the simple
S_{i}
, then
\mathbb{F}\Upphi_{\upalpha}\left(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}\right)
is filtered by
\mathbb{F}\Upphi_{\upalpha}(S_{i}).
It is known that
S_{i}
is supported only at the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
, thus for
\mathfrak{p}\neq\mathfrak{m}

\Upphi_{\upalpha}(S_{i})_{\mathfrak{p}}=\mathbf{R}\text{Hom}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}(\uptau_{\upalpha},S_{i})_{\mathfrak{p}}=\mathbf{R}\text{Hom}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{\mathfrak{p}}}({\uptau_{\upalpha}}_{\mathfrak{p}},{S_{i}}_{\mathfrak{p}})=0.

So,
\mathbb{F}\Upphi_{\upalpha}(S_{i})=\Upphi_{\upalpha}(S_{i})\otimes^{\phantom{L}}_{\!\operatorname{\mathrm{A}}}\!{\mathbb{M}^{*}}
is also supported at only the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}.
Thus,
\mathbb{F}\Upphi_{\upalpha}(S_{i})_{\hat{\Lambda}}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{fl}}\hat{\Lambda}
. Since by Iyama–Reiten
[
IR
]

\operatorname{\mathsf{fl}}\hat{\Lambda}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{fl}}\Lambda
, then
\mathbb{F}\Upphi_{\upalpha}(S_{i})
is a finite length
\Lambda
-module supported only at the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}.

∎

Recall from
\lx@sectionsign\ref{specific hyperplanes}
the associated hyperplane arrangements
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{aff}}.

Lemma 3.5
.

If
\upalpha
is an atom in
\mathcal{H}
or
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{aff}}
, then
\mathrm{H}^{t}(\Upphi^{-1}_{\upalpha}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}))=0
for all but one
t.

Proof.

\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}
is filtered by the simple
S_{i}
. By the torsion pairs described in
[
HW
]
, since
\upalpha
is an atom we know that
\Upphi^{-1}_{\upalpha}(S_{i})
is only in one homological degree. Thus
\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{-1}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i})
is in one homological degree only.
∎

The following is also well known.

Lemma 3.6
.

With notation as above, the following statements hold.

(1)

If
Z
is a
\operatorname{\mathsf{fl}}\hat{\Lambda}
-module, then the natural map
Z\otimes_{\hat{\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}\otimes_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}\to Z
is an isomorphism of
\hat{\Lambda}
-modules.

(2)

If
a\in\mathop{\rm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{fl}}}}(\operatorname{mod}\hat{\Lambda}),
then
a\otimes_{\hat{\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}\otimes_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}\cong a
in
\mathop{\rm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{fl}}}}(\operatorname{mod}\hat{\Lambda})
.

Proof.

(1) Consider the following functors between categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{fl}}\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{fl}}\Lambda_{\mathfrak{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{fl}}\hat{\Lambda},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}\Lambda_{\mathfrak{m}_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{m}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{m}}}\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{\Lambda}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{m}}}\Lambda_{\mathfrak{m}_{\Lambda}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\hat{\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{m}}}}

where the bottom left functor is fully faithful by
[
IR
]
. By
[
DW1
, 2.15]
,
[
IR
, p1100]
, the rightmost functors are an equivalence of categories, thus composing we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{fl}}\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{fl}}\hat{\Lambda}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\hat{\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}}

The claimed morphism is the counit, and this is an isomorphism since the right adjoint functor
\otimes_{\hat{\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}
is fully faithful.

(2) By
[
IR
, 2.5]
there is an equivalence of categories
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{\mathsf{fl}}\hat{\Lambda})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathop{\rm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{fl}}}}(\operatorname{mod}\hat{\Lambda})
, so

a\cong\cdots\to a_{i+1}\to a_{i}\to a_{i-1}\to\cdots

with each
a_{i}
a finite length
\hat{\Lambda}
-module. Since by Lemma
3.6
(
1
)
a_{i}\otimes_{\hat{\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}\otimes_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}\cong a_{i}
via the counit, it follows that
a\cong a\otimes_{\hat{\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}\otimes_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}
as complexes, as required.
∎

4.
Construction of Bimodule Cones

In the local Zariski setting of
2.2
where
\Lambda
is derived equivalent to
U,
this section uses bimodule cones to construct endofunctors of
U.

Proposition 4.1
.

For any choice of
(\upalpha,i)
as in Setup
3.1
, there exists
{}_{\Lambda}C_{\Lambda}=C_{\upalpha,i}
in the derived category of
\Lambda
-
\Lambda
bimodules, such that, given the functorial diagram

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to116.12pt{\vbox to47.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 16.24768pt\lower-23.54353pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{35.38687pt}{-18.23831pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array},

setting
\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\Lambda}(C_{\Lambda},-)
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}^{*}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}-\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\Lambda}C_{\Lambda},
there are functorial triangles

(1)

\Uppsi_{\upalpha,i}\circ\Uppsi_{\upalpha,i}^{\operatorname{RA}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}_{\Lambda}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}_{\upalpha,i}\to

(2)

\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}^{*}_{\upalpha,i}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}_{\Lambda}\to\Uppsi_{\upalpha,i}\circ\Uppsi_{\upalpha,i}^{\operatorname{LA}}\to.

Proof.

(1) We expand the diagram in (
4.1
) to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{D}\left(\operatorname{\mathrm{A}}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{D}(\hat{\Lambda})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{D}(\Lambda)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Upphi_{\upalpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{\operatorname{RA}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{\operatorname{RA}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}^{\operatorname{RA}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{F}^{\operatorname{RA}}}

where

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}=\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}}({}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}},-)

\displaystyle\cong-\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}_{i}}\!{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrm{H}^{\operatorname{RA}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}({}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}}{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}},-)

\displaystyle\Upphi_{\upalpha}=\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}({}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{A}}}\uptau_{\upalpha},-)

\displaystyle\cong-\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}{\uptau^{*}_{\upalpha}}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{A}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{\operatorname{RA}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{A}}}({}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}\uptau^{*}_{\upalpha},-)

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}=\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{A}}}({}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\mathbb{M},-)

\displaystyle\cong-\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\mathbb{M}^{*}_{\hat{\Lambda}}

\displaystyle\qquad\mathbb{F}^{\operatorname{RA}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}\text{Hom}_{\hat{\Lambda}}({}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\mathbb{M}^{*},-)

\displaystyle\mathrm{F}=\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\hat{\Lambda}}({}_{\Lambda}\hat{\Lambda},-)

\displaystyle\cong-\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrm{F}^{\operatorname{RA}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}\text{Hom}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}({}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda},-)

We will construct
\Uppsi_{\upalpha,i}\circ\Uppsi_{\upalpha,i}^{\operatorname{RA}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}_{\Lambda}
by exhibiting a certain bimodule map. First, the ring homomorphism
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}\to\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}
is a map of
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}
-
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}
bimodules and thus induces a natural transformation
\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}({}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}},-)\to\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}},-)
. But this is equal to

\displaystyle=\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}({}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}\!\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}_{i}}{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}},-)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}})}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}\circ\mathrm{H}^{\operatorname{RA}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}_{\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}})}

We next construct a natural transformation
\Upphi_{\upalpha}\circ\mathrm{H}\circ\mathrm{H}^{\operatorname{RA}}\circ\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{\operatorname{RA}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}_{\operatorname{D}(A)}.
Tensoring
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}\to\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}
on the left by
\uptau_{\upalpha}
and on the right by
\uptau^{*}_{\upalpha}
gives rise to a bimodule map

\operatorname{\mathrm{A}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}\uptau^{*}_{\upalpha}\cong\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}\,\uptau^{*}_{\upalpha}\to\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}\,\uptau^{*}_{\upalpha}

which induces the claimed
\Upphi_{\upalpha}\circ\mathrm{H}\circ\mathrm{H}^{\operatorname{RA}}\circ\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{\operatorname{RA}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}_{\operatorname{D}(A)}.

Now, applying the same trick for
\mathbb{F},
setting
Z_{\upalpha,i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}\otimes_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}^{\bf{L}}\,\uptau_{\upalpha}^{*}\otimes_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}^{\bf{L}}\mathbb{M}^{*},
then

Composing this with the ring homomorphism
\Lambda\to\hat{\Lambda}
thus gives a bimodule map

\Lambda\to{}_{\Lambda}\hat{\Lambda}\otimes\left(\mathbb{M}\otimes\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes Z_{\upalpha,i}\right)\otimes\hat{\Lambda}_{\Lambda}.

Taking the cone in the derived category of
\Lambda
-
\Lambda
bimodules gives a triangle

C\to{}_{\Lambda}\Lambda_{\Lambda}\to{}_{\Lambda}\hat{\Lambda}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\left(\mathbb{M}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}Z_{\upalpha,i}\right)\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}_{\Lambda}\to C[1].

(4.F)

This induces a functorial triangle
\Uppsi_{\upalpha,i}\circ\Uppsi_{\upalpha,i}^{\operatorname{RA}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}_{\Lambda}\to\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}({}_{\Lambda}C_{\Lambda},-)\to.
Defining
\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}_{\upalpha,i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}({}_{\Lambda}C_{\Lambda},-),
yields the result.

(2) (
4.F
) also induces a functorial triangle
-\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\Lambda}C_{\Lambda}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}\to\Uppsi_{\upalpha,i}\circ\Uppsi_{\upalpha,i}^{\operatorname{LA}}\to,
thus defining
\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}_{\upalpha,i}^{*}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}-\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\Lambda}C_{\Lambda}
gives the claim.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

In the proof of Proposition
4.1
,
Z_{\upalpha,i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}\otimes_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}^{\bf{L}}\,\uptau_{\upalpha}^{*}\otimes_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}^{\bf{L}}\mathbb{M}^{*}
. We will use this notation below.

Remark 4.3
.

\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}_{\upalpha,i}^{*}
is the left adjoint of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}_{\upalpha,i}.
Later, in Remark
6.14
we will prove that it is the inverse.

5.
The Key Commutative Diagram

Under the Zariski local Setup
2.2
and given any choice of
(\upalpha,i)
as defined in Setup
3.1
, this section proves that the functors
\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}_{\upalpha,i}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}^{*}_{\upalpha,i}
constructed in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{twistfun}
intertwine with the known equivalences in the complete local setting, through a key commutative diagram.

Given a choice of
(\upalpha,i),
in particular with associated homomorphism
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}\to\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i},
set
I_{i}
to be the kernel of the homomorphism, which is a two-sided ideal of
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}.

Claim 5.1
.

Given an atom
\upalpha\colon C_{+}\to D
and a wall
i
of
D,
set
M=N_{D}
so that
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M)
and
\upnu_{\kern-1.0pti}\kern-2.0pt\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}=\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{R}}(\upnu_{i}M).
Then

I_{i}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(M,\upnu_{i}M)\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\upnu_{\kern-1.0pti}\kern-2.0pt\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(\upnu_{i}M,M)

as
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}
-bimodules.

Proof.

The proof of
[
DW1
, 5.10(1)]
shows

I_{i}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(M,\upnu_{i}M)\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\upnu_{\kern-1.0pti}\kern-2.0pt\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(\upnu_{i}M,M)

as right modules by showing that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(M,\upnu_{i}M)\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\upnu_{\kern-1.0pti}\kern-2.0pt\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(\upnu_{i}M,M)
is concentrated in degree zero. Hence truncating in the category of bimodules there is an isomorphism of
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}
-bimodules

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(M,\upnu_{i}M)\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\upnu_{\kern-1.0pti}\kern-2.0pt\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(\upnu_{i}M,M)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(M,\upnu_{i}M)\otimes_{\upnu_{\kern-1.0pti}\kern-2.0pt\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(\upnu_{i}M,M),

so it suffices to show that

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(M,\upnu_{i}M)\otimes_{\upnu_{\kern-1.0pti}\kern-2.0pt\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(\upnu_{i}M,M)\cong\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}e_{i}\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}

as
B
-bimodules.

Adding
\frac{M}{M_{i}}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}}\frac{M}{M_{i}}
approximately to the exchange sequence (
2.B
), there exists an exact sequence

0\to\upnu_{i}M\to V\xrightarrow{b}M,

(5.A)

where
b
is an
\left(\mathrm{add}\frac{M}{M_{i}}\right)
-approximation of
M
. We now claim that applying
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(\upnu_{i}M,-)
to (
5.A
) gives an exact sequence

0\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(\upnu_{i}M,\upnu_{i}M)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(\upnu_{i}M,V)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(\upnu_{i}M,M)\to 0.

(5.B)

To see this, set
\Gamma=\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{R}}\left(\frac{M}{M_{i}}\right)
and
\mathbb{G}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}\left(\frac{M}{M_{i}},-\right)
, then since
b
is an
\left(\mathrm{add}\frac{M}{M_{i}}\right)
-approximation of
M,

0\to\mathbb{G}(\upnu_{i}M)\to\mathbb{G}V\to\mathbb{G}M\to 0

is exact. Applying
\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma}(\mathbb{G}(\upnu_{i}M),-)
and dropping Hom’s gives a commutative diagram

\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to359.04pt{\vbox to54.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 61.23169pt\lower-48.51225pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-58.89857pt}{-2.25555pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{173.24966pt}{-23.08957pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}

in which the top line is exact.

By e.g
[
IW2
, 6.10(2)]
,
\operatorname{End}_{\Gamma}(\mathbb{G}(\upnu_{i}M))\cong\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal{R}}(\upnu_{i}M)\in\mathrm{CM}\mathcal{R},
so

\operatorname{\mathsf{fl}}_{\mathcal{R}}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\Gamma}(\mathbb{G}(\upnu_{i}M),\mathbb{G}(\upnu_{i}M))=0,

by
[
IW2
, 2.7]
. Since
\mathcal{R}
is isolated,
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}=0.
Thus the bottom line is also exact, as claimed.

Since (
5.B
) is exact, applying
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(M,\upnu_{i}M)\otimes_{\upnu_{\kern-1.0pti}\kern-2.0pt\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}-
gives an exact sequence in the top line of the following commutative diagram

{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{292.48827pt}{-49.08138pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}

whilst applying
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(M,-)
to (
5.A
) gives the bottom line (see
[
W1
, A.7(3)]
). The leftmost vertical maps are isomorphisms since
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(\upnu_{i}M,\upnu_{i}M)
and
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(\upnu_{i}M,V)
are projective
\upnu_{\kern-1.0pti}\kern-2.0pt\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}
-modules.
Thus

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(M,\upnu_{i}M)\otimes_{\upnu_{\kern-1.0pti}\kern-2.0pt\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(\upnu_{i}M,M)\cong\ker(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}\to\tfrac{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}e_{i}\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}})=\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}e_{i}\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}=I_{i}

via the map

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(M,\upnu_{i}M)\otimes_{\upnu_{\kern-1.0pti}\kern-2.0pt\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(\upnu_{i}M,M)\to I_{i}

sending
a\otimes b\mapsto b\circ a.

Since this clearly a bimodule map, thus bimodule isomorphism, the statement follows.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

There is a functorial isomorphism

\Upphi_{\upalpha}\circ\Upphi_{i}^{2}\circ\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{-1}\cong\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{A}}}(\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}I_{i}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}\uptau_{\upalpha}^{*},-).

Proof.

For the
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}
-bimodule
I_{i}
defined by the natural exact sequence
I_{i}\to\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}\to\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i},
by Claim
5.1

\Upphi_{i}\circ\Upphi_{i}\cong\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}(I_{i},-).
So,

\displaystyle\Upphi_{\upalpha}\circ\Upphi_{i}^{2}\circ\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{A}}}(\uptau_{\upalpha},-)\circ\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}(I_{i},-)\circ\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{A}}}(\uptau_{\upalpha}^{*},-)

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{A}}}(\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}I_{i}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}\uptau_{\upalpha}^{*},-),

as required.
∎

The point of the following result is that we can view the functor
\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}_{\upalpha,i}
as a lift of the complete local equivalence
\Upphi_{\upalpha}\circ\Upphi_{i}^{2}\circ\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{-1}.

Proposition 5.3
.

Under the Zariski local setup (
2.2
), the following hold.

(1)

There exists a triangle in the derived category of
\hat{\Lambda}
-
\hat{\Lambda}
bimodules

\mathbb{M}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}I_{i}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}\uptau^{*}_{\upalpha}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\mathbb{M}^{*}\to{}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\xrightarrow{h}\mathbb{M}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}Z_{\upalpha,i}.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-8.69273pt}{-23.73123pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{{{{}}{

{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 393.7 -46.69 L 393.7 -11.81}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{-1.0}{1.0}{0.0}{284.52759pt}{-33.94614pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{288.0606pt}{-23.73123pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}

Proof.

(1) The result is implied by tensoring the exact sequence
I_{i}\to\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}\to\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i},
using the isomorphisms
\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes\uptau_{\upalpha}^{*}\cong{}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{A}}}\!\operatorname{\mathrm{A}}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{A}}}
and
\mathbb{M}\otimes\mathbb{M}^{*}\cong{}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{\Lambda}}.

(2) Observe that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}_{\upalpha,i}\circ\,\mathrm{F}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\hat{\Lambda}}({}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}C\otimes_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}^{\bf{L}}\hat{\Lambda},-),

\displaystyle\mathrm{F}\circ(\mathbb{F}\circ\Upphi_{\upalpha}\circ\Upphi^{2}_{i}\circ\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{-1}\circ\mathbb{F}^{-1})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\hat{\Lambda}}({}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}\otimes_{\hat{\Lambda}}^{\bf{L}}\mathbb{M}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}I_{i}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}\uptau^{*}_{\upalpha}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\mathbb{M}^{*},-),

thus it suffices to prove that
C\otimes\hat{\Lambda}\cong\hat{\Lambda}\otimes\mathbb{M}\otimes\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes I_{i}\otimes\uptau_{\upalpha}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{M}^{*}
in the derived category of
\Lambda
-
\hat{\Lambda}
bimodules, where to ease notation, we have written
\otimes
instead of
\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{.}.

Tensoring (
4.F
) by
\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\Lambda}\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{\Lambda}}
gives

{}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}C\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\to{}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}\Lambda\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\xrightarrow{f\otimes\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}}{}^{\phantom{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\mathbb{M}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}Z_{\upalpha,i}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\to

(5.D)

Since
\upalpha
is an atom, by Lemma
3.5

\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{-1}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i})
is in one degree only, and by the proof it has finite length. Since
\mathbb{F}
is induced from a Morita equivalence,
\Upphi^{-1}\mathbb{F}^{-1}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i})
is also one degree only, and has finite length.

Now,
\Upphi^{-1}_{\upalpha}\mathbb{F}^{-1}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i})=\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}\,\uptau_{\upalpha}^{*}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\mathbb{M}^{*}
as one sided modules, which implies that
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}\,\uptau_{\upalpha}^{*}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\mathbb{M}^{*}
is one degree only and in that degree the cohomology is finite dimensional.

Now, the natural map
\uppsi~\colon Z_{\upalpha,i}\otimes_{\hat{\Lambda}}(\hat{\Lambda}\otimes_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda})\to{}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}{Z_{\upalpha,i}}_{\hat{\Lambda}}
sending
z\otimes r\otimes s\mapsto zrs
is a bijection by Lemma
3.6
(
2
), so since this is clearly a bimodule homomorphism, it follows that
Z_{\upalpha,i}\otimes\hat{\Lambda}\otimes\hat{\Lambda}\cong Z_{\upalpha,i}
as
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}
-
\hat{\Lambda}
bimodules.

Since the rightmost vertical maps are isomorphisms, it follows that so is the leftmost, and thus
{}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}C\otimes\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\cong{}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}\otimes\mathbb{M}\otimes\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes I_{i}\otimes\uptau^{*}_{\upalpha}\otimes\mathbb{M}^{*}_{\hat{\Lambda}}
, as required.
∎

.

Proof.

Take
\operatorname{LA}
of
5.3
(
2
), using Remark
4.3
to see that
\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}_{\upalpha,i}^{*}
is left adjoint to
\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}_{\upalpha,i}.

∎

To set notation, given
(\upalpha,i)
in the finite arrangement
\mathcal{H},
say
\upalpha\colon C_{+}\to D.
Write
\hat{\Lambda}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{con}}}
for the contraction algebra in chamber
C_{+}
and
\hat{\Gamma}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{con}}}
for the contraction algebra in chamber
D.
Mimicking the introduction and Proposition
5.3
, consider the composition

\mathrm{J}_{\upalpha,i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\hat{\Lambda}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{con}}})\xrightarrow[\sim]{F_{\upalpha}}\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\hat{\Gamma}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{con}}})\xrightarrow[\sim]{F_{i}^{2}}\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\hat{\Gamma}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{con}}})\xrightarrow[\sim]{F_{\upalpha}^{-1}}\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\hat{\Lambda}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{con}}}),

where
F_{i}
and
F_{\upalpha}
are the standard equivalences in
[
A
]
.

The following lifts results from
[
A
]
to the Zariski local setting.

Corollary 5.5
.

For any choice of
(\upalpha,i)
in the finite arrangement
\mathcal{H},
the following diagram commutes

\operatorname{D}(\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{con}})
\operatorname{D}(\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{con}})
\operatorname{D}(\Lambda)
\operatorname{D}(\Lambda)
\scriptstyle\mathrm{J}_{\upalpha,i}
\scriptstyle\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}_{\upalpha,i}

The top diagram commutes by
[

]
and the bottom diagram is Proposition

(

).
∎

6.
One and Two sided tilting

In this section, we will prove that for any
(\upalpha,i),
the functor
\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}_{\upalpha,i}\colon\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\Lambda)\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\Lambda)
is an equivalence.

6.1.
Tilting Generalities

Throughout this subsection,
S
is a commutative Noetherian ring, and
\Gamma
is a module-finite
S
-algebra.

Lemma 6.1
.

If
M\in\operatorname{Mod}S
, then
M=0
if and only if
M_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}\hat{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}=0
for all
\mathfrak{p}\in\Spec S.

Proof.

By
[
AM
, Proposition 3.8 ]

M=0
iff
M_{\mathfrak{p}}=0
for all
\mathfrak{p}\in\Spec S.
By faithful flatness
[
M
, Theorem 7.2]
, this is to equivalent to
M_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}\hat{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}=0
for all
\mathfrak{p}\in\Spec S.

∎

Lemma 6.2
.

If
T\in\mathop{\rm{K}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{proj}\Gamma),
then there are isomorphisms

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\Gamma)}(T_{\Gamma},T_{\Gamma})\otimes_{S}S_{\mathfrak{p}}

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\Gamma_{\mathfrak{p}})}(T_{\mathfrak{p}},T_{\mathfrak{p}})

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\Gamma_{\mathfrak{p}})}(T_{\mathfrak{p}},T_{\mathfrak{p}})\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}\hat{S}

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\hat{\Gamma})}(T_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}\hat{S},T_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}\hat{S})

Proof.

This is Iyama-Reiten, proof of (3) and (4) in
[
IR
, Theorem 3.1]
.
∎

Definition 6.3
.

A one-sided tilting complex for
\Gamma
is an object
T\in\mathop{\rm{K}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{proj}\Gamma)
such that

(1)

\operatorname{Hom}(T,T[t])=0
for all
t\neq 0

(2)

If
x\in\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Mod}\Gamma)
with
\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\Gamma}(T,x)=0
, then
x\cong 0.

By
[
K
, p.80]
Definition
6.3
is equivalent to the other definitions in the literature.

Lemma 6.4
.

Given
T\in\mathop{\rm{K}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{proj}\Gamma),
the following are equivalent.

(1)

T
is one-sided tilting complex

(2)

T\otimes_{S}S_{\mathfrak{p}}
is one-sided tilting
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{p}}
-complex for all
\mathfrak{p}\in\Spec S

(3)

T\otimes_{S}S_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes\hat{S}
is one-sided tilting
\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathfrak{p}}
-complex for all
\mathfrak{p}\in\Spec S

Proof.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2). This holds by e.g
[
IR
, Lemma 2.7]
.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(3). We show
T_{\mathfrak{p}}
is tilting implies
A=T_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}\hat{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is tilting. It is clear, since
\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}\hat{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is exact, taking projectives to projectives, that
A\in\mathop{\rm{K}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{proj}\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathfrak{p}}).

We know that
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\Gamma_{p})}(T_{\mathfrak{p}},T_{\mathfrak{p}}[t])=0
for all
t\neq 0
by assumption. Thus by Lemma
6.2
, for all
t\neq 0
we have

0=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\Gamma_{\mathfrak{p}})}(T_{\mathfrak{p}},T_{\mathfrak{p}}[t])\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}\hat{S}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\hat{\Gamma})}(T_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}\hat{S},T_{\mathfrak{p}}[t]\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}\hat{S})=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\hat{\Gamma})}(A,A[t]).

Now let
x\in\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Mod}\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathfrak{p}})
such that
\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(A,x)=0.
Then for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle 0=\mathrm{H}^{t}(\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(A,x))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathfrak{p}})}(A,x[t])

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathfrak{p}})}(T_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}\hat{S}_{\mathfrak{p}},x[t])

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\Gamma_{\mathfrak{p}})}(T_{\mathfrak{p}},\operatorname{res}(x)[t]).

Thus
\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}_{\Gamma_{\mathfrak{p}}}(T_{\mathfrak{p}},\operatorname{res}(x))=0,
so since
T_{\mathfrak{p}}
is tilting,
\operatorname{res}(x)\cong 0.
Since restriction of scalars is exact,
x\cong 0

(3)
\Rightarrow
(1). By assumption

0=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\hat{\Gamma})}(T_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}\hat{S},T_{\mathfrak{p}}[t]\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}\hat{S})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\hat{\Gamma})}(T,T[t])\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}\hat{S}.

Thus
\operatorname{Hom}(T,T[t])=0
for all
t\neq 0
, since
-\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}\hat{S}
is faithfully flat
[
M
, Theorem 7.2]
.

By assumption
T\in\mathop{\rm{K}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{proj}\Gamma).
Let
x\in\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Mod}\Gamma)
such that
\operatorname{{\mathbf{R}}Hom}(T,x)=0.
Then for all
t\in\mathbb{Z},\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\Gamma)}(T,x[t])=0.
Thus, tensoring by
\otimes{S_{\mathfrak{p}}},
using Lemma
6.2

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\Gamma)}(T,x[t])\otimes_{S}S_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}\hat{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathfrak{p}})}(\hat{T}_{\mathfrak{p}},\hat{x}_{\mathfrak{p}}[t]).

Since
\hat{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is tilting,
\hat{x}_{\mathfrak{p}}\cong 0.
Thus, since
-\otimes S_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
-\otimes\hat{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}
are exact, for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

\mathrm{H}^{t}(x)\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}S_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}\hat{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}\cong\mathrm{H}^{t}(x\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}S_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}\hat{S}_{\mathfrak{p}})=0.

But
\otimes_{S_{\mathfrak{p}}}\hat{S}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is faithfully flat, so by Lemma
6.1

\mathrm{H}^{t}(x)\cong 0
for all
t\in\mathbb{Z},
so
x\cong 0
as claimed.
∎

Corollary 6.5
.

Suppose
T\in\mathop{\rm{K}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{proj}\Gamma)
and choose
\mathfrak{m}\in\operatorname{Max}S
. If
T_{\mathfrak{p}}
is tilting for all
\mathfrak{p}\neq\mathfrak{m}
and
T_{\mathfrak{m}}\otimes\hat{S}
is tilting, then
T
is tilting.

Proof.

The proof follows from Lemma
6.4
by considering the two cases, one when the prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
is not equal the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
and another when
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{m}.

∎

6.2.
One sided tilting on
\Lambda

We now revert to setting
2.2
, where
\Lambda
is derived equivalent to
U
where
f\colon U\to\Spec R
is a flopping contraction and
R
is an isolated cDV. This subsection proves that
C_{\Lambda}\in\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda)
constructed in Proposition
4.1
is a one-sided tilting complex.

Recall that the setup
3.1
defines
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}
and
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}
.

Lemma 6.6
.

If
y\in\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{mod}\operatorname{\mathrm{B}})
is perfect, then
y\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}
is perfect.

Proof.

The exact sequence
0\to I_{i}\to\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}\to\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}\to 0
gives a triangle,
y\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}I_{i}\to y\to y\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}
. Now,
-\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}I_{i}
is an equivalence by
5.1
, so
y\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}I_{i}
is perfect. By the 2 out of 3 property
y\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}
is perfect.
∎

Definition 6.7
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a triangulated category. Then we say an object
A\in\mathcal{T}
is homologically finite if for any object
B\in\mathcal{T},
all
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{T}}(A,B[i])
are trivial except for a finite number of
i\in\mathbb{Z}.

By
[
IW4
, Proposition 2.18]
, for module-finite algebras, being a perfect complex is equivalent to being a homologically finite complex.

Proposition 6.8
.

C_{\Lambda}\in\mathop{\rm{K}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{proj}\Lambda).

Proof.

The exact sequence
0\to I_{i}\to\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}\to\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}\to 0
induces a triangle

in
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{mod}\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}).

Since
\Upphi_{i},\Upphi_{\upalpha},\mathbb{F}
are equivalences, then
\Upphi_{i}^{2}\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{-1}\mathbb{F}^{-1}(\hat{\Lambda})
and
\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{-1}\mathbb{F}^{-1}(\hat{\Lambda})
are perfect as complexes of right
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}
-modules. By the 2 out of 3 property,
(\hat{\Lambda}\otimes\mathbb{M}\otimes\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i})_{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}}
is thus perfect as a complex of right
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}
-modules.

Now set

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\hat{\Lambda}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\mathbb{M}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}_{i}}\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}\uptau_{\upalpha}^{*}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\mathbb{M}^{*}

\displaystyle=\hat{\Lambda}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\mathbb{M}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}Z_{\upalpha,i},

(by definition of
Z_{\upalpha,i}
)

so (
4.F
) becomes

C\to{}_{\Lambda}\Lambda_{\Lambda}\to{}_{\Lambda}M\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}_{\Lambda}\to C[1].
Now observe that

M_{\hat{\Lambda}}\cong\mathbb{F}\Upphi_{\upalpha}\left(\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{-1}\mathbb{F}^{-1}(\hat{\Lambda})\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}\right).

Since
\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{-1}\mathbb{F}^{-1}(\hat{\Lambda})
is perfect by above,
\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{-1}\mathbb{F}^{-1}(\hat{\Lambda})\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}{\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}
is perfect by Lemma
6.6
. Since
\Upphi_{\upalpha},\mathbb{F}
are equivalences,
M\in\mathop{\rm{K}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{proj}\hat{\Lambda}).

To show that
M\otimes\hat{\Lambda}_{\Lambda}\in\mathop{\rm{K}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{proj}\Lambda)
, note that the complex
M\in\mathop{\rm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{fl}}}}(\operatorname{mod}\hat{\Lambda})
since
\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}_{i}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{fl}}\operatorname{\mathrm{B}}.
Thus

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\Lambda)}(M\otimes\hat{\Lambda}_{\Lambda},x[t])

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{03 0f 06 march 2023}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\hat{\Lambda})}(M\otimes\hat{\Lambda}\otimes_{\Lambda}\hat{\Lambda},\hat{x}_{\mathfrak{m}}[t])=0

(since
\operatorname{Supp}N\otimes\hat{\Lambda}=\{\mathfrak{m}\}
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{04 0f 24 january 2023}\eqref{04 0f 24 january 2023 two}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\hat{\Lambda})}(M,\hat{x}_{\mathfrak{m}}[t])=0

Since
M\in\mathop{\rm{K}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{proj}\hat{\Lambda})
by above, it is homologically finite. Thus, by above,
M\otimes\hat{\Lambda}
is also homologically finite, which in turn implies it is perfect. Considering (
4.F
), by the 2 out of 3 property,
C_{\Lambda}\in\mathop{\rm{K}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{proj}\Lambda),
since both
\Lambda
and
M\otimes\hat{\Lambda}
are.
∎

Lemma 6.9
.

As a right
\Lambda
-module,
\operatorname{Ext}^{t}_{\Lambda}(C_{\Lambda},C_{\Lambda})=0
for all
t\neq 0.
Thus C is one sided tilting complex.

Proof.

As a triangle of right
\Lambda
-modules, (
4.F
) can be rewritten as

C_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}\to\Lambda_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}\to\hat{\Lambda}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\left(\mathbb{M}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}Z_{\upalpha,i}\right)\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}\to,

which when localized at a prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
becomes

C_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}\otimes_{R}R_{\mathfrak{p}}\to\Lambda_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}\otimes_{R}R_{\mathfrak{p}}\to\left(\hat{\Lambda}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\mathbb{M}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}\mathrm{A}}}\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\mathrm{B}}}\mathrm{B}}}Z_{\upalpha,i}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\hat{\Lambda}}\hat{\Lambda}_{\operatorname{\vphantom{\hat{\Lambda}}\Lambda}}\right)\otimes_{R}R_{\mathfrak{p}}\to

(6.A)

When
\mathfrak{p}\neq\mathfrak{m},
since
\hat{\Lambda}\otimes\mathbb{M}\otimes\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes Z_{\upalpha,i}\otimes\hat{\Lambda}
is supported at only the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
,
(\hat{\Lambda}\otimes\mathbb{M}\otimes\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes Z_{\upalpha,i}\otimes\hat{\Lambda})_{\mathfrak{p}}=0.
Thus
C_{\mathfrak{p}}\cong\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}
, which is tilting in
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}).

When
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{m},
by the last line in the proof of Proposition
5.3
(
2
), there is an isomorphism of
\Lambda
-
\hat{\Lambda}
bimodules

C\otimes_{\Lambda}\hat{\Lambda}\cong\hat{\Lambda}\otimes\mathbb{M}\otimes\uptau_{\upalpha}\otimes I_{i}\otimes\uptau_{\upalpha}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{M}^{*}.

Considering these as simply right
\hat{\Lambda}
-modules, it follows that

C\otimes_{R}R_{\mathfrak{m}}\otimes\hat{R}\cong\mathbb{F}\Upphi_{\upalpha}\Upphi_{i}^{2}\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{-1}\mathbb{F}^{-1}(\hat{\Lambda}).

Since
\hat{\Lambda}
is tilting in
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\hat{\Lambda})
and
\mathbb{F},\Upphi_{\upalpha},\Upphi_{i}
are all equivalences, it follows that
C\otimes_{R}R_{\mathfrak{m}}\otimes\hat{R}
is also tilting. Since
C_{\Lambda}\in\mathop{\rm{K}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{proj}\Lambda)
by Proposition
6.8
, using Corollary
6.5
it follows that
C
is a one-sided tilting complex.
∎

6.3.
Two sided tilting on
\Lambda

Definition 6.10
.

A bimodule
{}_{\Gamma}X_{\Lambda},
is called a two-sided tilting complex if

(1)

\Gamma\to\operatorname{End}_{\operatorname{D}(\Lambda)}(X,X)
induced by
-\otimes_{\Gamma}^{\bf{L}}X
is an isomorphism.

(2)

X_{\Lambda}
is a one sided tilting complex in the sense of Definition
6.3
.

Theorem 6.11
.

{}_{\Lambda}C_{\Lambda}
is a two-sided tilting complex.

Proof.

We know
C_{\Lambda}
is one sided tilting by Lemma
6.9
. Thus it suffices to prove that

f\colon\Lambda\xrightarrow{-\otimes_{\Lambda}^{\bf{L}}C}\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\Lambda)}(C,C)

is an isomorphism. Isomorphisms of
R
-modules can be checked locally. So, it suffices to show that

\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}\xrightarrow{f_{\mathfrak{p}}=-\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}}C_{\mathfrak{p}}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}(\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}})}(C_{\mathfrak{p}},C_{\mathfrak{p}}),

(6.B)

is an isomorphism, for all
\mathfrak{p}\in\Spec R.

When
\mathfrak{p}\neq\mathfrak{m}
, considering the triangle (
4.F
),
{}_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}}C_{\mathfrak{p}_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}}}\cong{}_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}}\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}_{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}}},
thus
f_{\mathfrak{p}}
is an isomorphism.

When
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{m}
, now
f_{\mathfrak{m}}
is an isomorphism if and only if
\hat{f}_{\mathfrak{m}}=\hat{f}
is an isomorphism. By the universal property of completion
[
M
, p.57]
,
\hat{f}
is a continuous ring homomorphism such that

are bijections, and so is
\mathrm{G}^{-1}_{\upalpha,i}
, it follows that the composition
\hat{f}
is an isomorphism as required.
∎

The following is part of the main result of
[
R2
]
, specifically
[
R3
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Theorem 6.12
.

Let
\Gamma
and
\Lambda
be module finite
R
-algebras, and
X
a complex of
\Gamma
-
\Lambda
bimodules. The following are equivalent.

(1)

-\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\Gamma}X\colon\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Mod}\Gamma)\to\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{Mod}\Lambda)
is an equivalence.

(2)

-\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\Gamma}X
induces an equivalence
\mathop{\rm{K}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{proj}\Gamma)\to\mathop{\rm{K}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{proj}\Lambda).

(3)

-\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\Gamma}X
induces an equivalence
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{mod}\Gamma)\to\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda).

(4)

X
is a two sided tilting complex.

Corollary 6.13
.

For any choice
(\upalpha,i)
as in Setup
3.1
,
{}_{\Lambda}C_{\Lambda}
in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{twistfun}
is a two-sided tilting complex, giving rise to the autoequivalence
\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}_{\upalpha,i}
of
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda).

Proof.

{}_{\Lambda}C_{\Lambda}
as constructed in Proposition
4.1
is a two sided tilting complex on
\Lambda
by Theorem
6.11
. Thus
{}_{\Lambda}C_{\Lambda}
induces a derived autoequivalence on the triangulated category
\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{mod}\Lambda),
by applying Theorem
6.12
.
∎

6.4.
Corollaries

The following are immediate.

Remark 6.14
.

\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}_{\upalpha,i}^{-1}=\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}_{\upalpha,i}^{*},
since the adjoint to an equivalence is necessarily the inverse.

Notation 6.15
.

As in the proof of Theorem
6.11
, set

\mathrm{G}_{\upalpha,i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\operatorname{D}(\hat{\Lambda})\xrightarrow{\mathbb{F}^{-1}}\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{\mathrm{A}})\xrightarrow{\Upphi_{\upalpha}^{-1}}\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}})\xrightarrow{\Upphi_{i}^{2}}\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{\mathrm{B}})\xrightarrow{\Upphi_{\upalpha}}\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{\mathrm{A}})\xrightarrow{\mathbb{F}}\operatorname{D}(\hat{\Lambda}).

Proof.

) and Corollary

respectively. The second follows from the first since
\mathrm{G}_{\upalpha,i}

(
2
)

gives the desired result

∎

7.

\mathop{\rm{D}^{b}}\nolimits(\operatorname{coh}U)

In this section we study the twist functor defined in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{twistfun}
from a geometric viewpoint. We use this to produce a group action on the derived category of coherent sheaves of
U,
where the irreducible rational curves may or may not be individually floppable.

(2)

The inverse geometric twist functor
\operatorname{\mathsf{GeoTwist}}^{*}_{\upalpha,i}
can be defined similarly, by composing with
\operatorname{\mathsf{Twist}}^{*}_{\upalpha,i}.

Remark 7.2
.

Being compositions of equivalences,
\operatorname{\mathsf{GeoTwist}}_{\upalpha,i}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{GeoTwist}}^{*}_{\upalpha,i}
are also equivalences. Moreover
\operatorname{\mathsf{GeoTwist}}^{*}_{\upalpha,i}\cong\operatorname{\mathsf{GeoTwist}}_{\upalpha,i}^{-1}.

As the natural way to describe functors between derived categories of coherent sheaves of varieties, we now describe the functors
\operatorname{\mathsf{GeoTwist}}_{\upalpha,i}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{GeoTwist}}^{*}_{\upalpha,i}
as Fourier–Mukai transforms.

Since both
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{V}^{*}
are tilting bundles on
U,
adopting the commutative diagram in
[

set
\mathcal{V}\XBox\mathcal{V}^{*}=p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{V}\otimes^{\bf{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{U\times U}}p_{2}^{*}\mathcal{V}^{*}.
Then as in
[
BH
]
we recall that

\operatorname{End}_{U\times U}(\mathcal{V}\XBox\mathcal{V}^{*})=\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Lambda^{\mathrm{op}},

the enveloping algebra of
\Lambda
. Thus by basic theory there exists a derived equivalence
